What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, I suppose what finally made me come in is this constant tightness I've been dealing with. My neck, my shoulders, even my jaw—they're just tight almost all the time now. It feels like I'm constantly bracing for bad news, even when I'm just sitting at my desk at school or trying to watch TV at night. It just doesn't seem to let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any pain in these tight areas?\n\nPatient: No, not really pain. It's... well, it's mostly just stiff. I mean, my muscles feel tired from being clenched all day, but I wouldn't say it actually hurts. It's just that constant tension.\n\nDoctor: Are there any particular situations or times when this tightness feeling gets worse?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I haven't really noticed a specific pattern to it. You'd think it might be worse during the day when I'm teaching or dealing with the students, but... it's just kind of always there. \n\nEven on a Saturday morning when I have absolutely nothing going on, I still catch myself clenching my jaw, or I'll realize my shoulders are practically up by my ears. So, no, I wouldn't say there's a particular situation or time that sets it off. It's just a constant thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. By the time the last bell rings, I just feel completely wrung out. And it's in a way that... well, a decent night's sleep or a quiet Sunday doesn't really fix it. It just lingers. Even sitting down to grade a short stack of quizzes, it just feels like I'm pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really sad. I mean, I'm exhausted, like I said, and the worrying never really stops, but I wouldn't call it feeling down or depressed. Just... drained from all the tension, I guess. But no, I don't feel sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. It's like my mind just keeps jumping from one thing to another. I'll be worrying about whether the contractors missed something in the house, and then it immediately shifts to work—wondering if my lesson plans are slipping, or if the robotics kids are falling behind. And then... I just start worrying that I'm disappointing my wife and family by not keeping everything under control. It just never really stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I can tell myself to stop and just focus on the class in front of me, but... all it takes is one small thought. Like remembering a late invoice, or noticing a student falling behind. It just sets off this whole chain of what-ifs, and once that starts, I really can't shut it down.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or situations because of your worrying or tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It's definitely getting in the way of things. I've started falling behind on my lesson prep and grading for school. And outside of work... well, I actually gave up parts of the community-theater stuff I usually manage. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore, so I had to step back.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any headaches?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I've had any headaches. I mean, my mind is always racing and I feel stressed, but it doesn't actually turn into physical pain in my head. So, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are intrusive or unwanted?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say they're unwanted or, like, out of nowhere. It's just the normal stuff I have to deal with—school, bills, the theater group—but it just spirals. It's not like random or weird thoughts popping into my head, just... the regular worries, but on overdrive, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I just feel wound up most of the day, honestly. It's like... I'm just waiting for something else to go wrong. I'll catch myself pacing around the classroom between periods, or checking my phone over and over for messages from the contractor. I just can't really seem to shake that on-edge feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense surge of anxiety or fear that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I have anything like that. It doesn't really hit me all at once or peak like you're describing. It's more of a... a constant, steady level of stress that just stays with me throughout the day. So, no sudden, intense surges, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your tension or worrying affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my wife knows I'm stressed out about things, but it hasn't caused any fights or anything like that. And with my friends or the people I work with at the school... no, things are pretty much the same. I guess I mostly just keep the worrying to myself, so it hasn't really gotten in the way of my relationships.\n\nDoctor: Have you had difficulty completing tasks or meeting deadlines due to your worrying or tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I actually have. It feels like I'm constantly bracing for bad news, even when I'm just sitting at my desk at school or trying to watch TV at night. I mean, my muscles feel tired from being clenched all day, but I wouldn't say it actually hurts. I mean, I haven't really noticed a specific pattern to it. You'd think it might be worse during the day when I'm teaching or dealing with the students, but... it's just kind of always there. \n\nEven on a Saturday morning when I have absolutely nothing going on, I still catch myself clenching my jaw, or I'll realize my shoulders are practically up by my ears. So, no, I wouldn't say there's a particular situation or time that sets it off. It's just a constant thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. By the time the last bell rings, I just feel completely wrung out. And it's in a way that... well, a decent night's sleep or a quiet Sunday doesn't really fix it. It just lingers. Even sitting down to grade a short stack of quizzes, it just feels like I'm pushing through mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: No, not really sad. I mean, I'm exhausted, like I said, and the worrying never really stops, but I wouldn't call it feeling down or depressed. Just... drained from all the tension, I guess. But no, I don't feel sad.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. It's like my mind just keeps jumping from one thing to another. I'll be worrying about whether the contractors missed something in the house, and then it immediately shifts to work—wondering if my lesson plans are slipping, or if the robotics kids are falling behind. And then... I just start worrying that I'm disappointing my wife and family by not keeping everything under control. It just never really stops.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having thoughts that repeat over and over and are difficult to stop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I can tell myself to stop and just focus on the class in front of me, but... all it takes is one small thought. Like remembering a late invoice, or noticing a student falling behind. And outside of work... well, I actually gave up parts of the community-theater stuff I usually manage. I just couldn't keep up with all of it anymore, so I had to step back.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any headaches?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't think I've had any headaches. I mean, my mind is always racing and I feel stressed, but it doesn't actually turn into physical pain in my head. It's just the normal stuff I have to deal with—school, bills, the theater group—but it just spirals. It's not like random or weird thoughts popping into my head, just... the regular worries, but on overdrive, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
